\section{Data and methodology}\label{data-and-methodology}

\subsection{Data}\label{data}

This study draws on four data sources: SA monetary policy surprises from
\citet{pirozhkova2024a}, South African economic data releases from
\textbf{add}, ZARONIA overnight index swaps (OIS) from the South African
Reserve Bank (SARB), and US monetary policy surprises from
\citet{jarocinski2024a}. The sample runs from April 2008 to November
2024, with the end date determined by the availability of ZARONIA OIS
data. All SARB announcement days within this period are included, with
limited exceptions where observations were dropped due to missing values
or release dates that did not coincide with announcement days.

The SA monetary policy surprises are summarised in
Figure~\ref{fig-sa_suprises}. A vast literature exists on the high
frequency identification of exogenous monetary policy shocks
\citep[see][]{kuttner2001b, cook1989, gurkaynak2005a, swanson2021a, miranda-agrippino2021}.
\citet{pirozhkova2024a} follow in this tradition isolating four
surprises from high frequency South African financial market data. These
are the traditional Target, Forward Guidance (FG) surprises, and the
less traditional Central Bank Information (CBI), and Country Risk (CR)
surprises. The target surprise account for the immediate response by
investors to a policy announcement, whilst forward guidance incorporates
responses to the communicated longer term path of policy. The CBI can be
viewed as a subset of the FG, which specifically focuses on the economy
\citep{jarocinski2024a}. The CR surprise is a novel addition by
\citet{pirozhkova2024a}, which details the role of the central bank in
responding to external shocks in a small open economy, such as South
Africa. For our purposes, these surprises provide the best available
estimates of monetary policy surprises in South Africa for analysing the
exchange rate and interest rate response to monetary policy surprises.
As a supplement our analysis of the response to South African news, by
using data on economic release - cpi, gdp, and unemployment. The
surprise to these news is defined as the difference between the actual
release and the forecasts from prominent analysts. To understand the
equivalent response to US surprises, we made use of the
\citet{jarocinski2024a} surprises (see Figure~\ref{fig-us_suprises}).
These are defined exactly as the \citet{pirozhkova2024a}, with the
exclusion of the CR surprise, and the inclusion of the Large-scale Asset
Purchases (LSAP) surprise. Lastly, the SARB recently undertook a reform
of its benchmark rate the JIBAR. The result of that process is the
ZARONIA OIS data, which is used to measure overnight interest rates in
South Africa (see Figure~\ref{fig-zaronia}). The 2, 5, and 10 year
tenors are of interest to us. These give a detailed picture of the term
structure of interest rates and their response to monetary policy
shocks. The key variables for the analysis are shown in
Figure~\ref{fig-joint_response}
